CISA KEV: CVE-2025-14733 — WatchGuard Firebox Out of Bounds Write Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-12-19 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

WatchGuard Fireware OS iked process contains an out of bounds write vulnerability in the OS iked process. This vulnerability may allow a remote unauthenticated attacker to execute arbitrary code and affects both the mobile user VPN with IKEv2 and the branch office VPN using IKEv2 when configured with a dynamic gateway peer. Vendor: WatchGuard, Product: Firebox. Federal patch due: 2025-12-26.

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-14733")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-14733")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

NosyDoor AppDomainManager hijack: UevAppMonitor.exe executing from non-standard path AIInstallation High
Hunts the NosyDoor stage-2 LOLBin: the legitimate UevAppMonitor.exe is normally only present in C:\Windows\System32, but LongNosedGoblin's dropper copies it to C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework so it loads a sibling .config that triggers AppDomainManager injection from SharedReg.dll. Execution of UevAppMonitor.exe from any path other than System32 is highly indicative of NosyDoor stage-2. Rationale: UevAppMonitor.exe is a real Microsoft UE-V binary that ships only in System32; ESET explicitly notes the dropper copies it to C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework, then loads SharedReg.dll through a crafted .config (AppDomainManager injection). Cross-checked against The Hacker News and SecurityWeek coverage which restate the same path / DLL. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2025/12/china-aligned-threat-group-uses-windows.html • https://www.securityweek.com/chinese-apt-longnosedgoblin-targeting-asian-governments/ • https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2025/12/18/eset-china-aligned-apt-group-policy/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1574/014/
ATT&CKT1574.014 T1218 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem
// LongNosedGoblin NosyDoor stage-2 — UevAppMonitor.exe is a legitimate Windows binary that *only* ships in System32. Execution from anywhere else (especially Microsoft.NET\Framework) means a copy was staged by the dropper to side-load SharedReg.dll via AppDomainManager.
let UevExec =
    DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where FileName =~ "UevAppMonitor.exe"
    | where not(FolderPath startswith @"C:\Windows\System32\")
    | where not(FolderPath startswith @"C:\Windows\SysWOW64\")
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine,
              InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
              InitiatingProcessFolderPath, SHA256;
let SharedRegLoad =
    DeviceImageLoadEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "UevAppMonitor.exe"
    | where FileName =~ "SharedReg.dll"
    | where not(FolderPath startswith @"C:\Windows\WinSxS\")
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256,
              InitiatingProcessFolderPath;
UevExec
| union SharedRegLoad
| order by Timestamp desc
// LongNosedGoblin NosyDoor stage-2 — legitimate UevAppMonitor.exe staged outside System32
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName endswith @"\UevAppMonitor.exe"
| where not(NewProcessName startswith @"C:\Windows\System32\")
| where not(NewProcessName startswith @"C:\Windows\SysWOW64\")
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName, ProcessId, TokenElevationType
| order by TimeGenerated desc

NosyDoor persistence: scheduled task 'OneDrive Reporting Task-S-1-5-21-' under Microsoft folder AIInstallation High
NosyDoor's dropper persists by creating a scheduled task with the literal prefix 'OneDrive Reporting Task-S-1-5-21-' followed by a random GUID, registered under the Microsoft task folder, executing the relocated UevAppMonitor.exe at startup. The naming pattern is article-unique and not used by Microsoft OneDrive itself. Rationale: ESET reports the dropper creates a Windows scheduled task literally named 'OneDrive Reporting Task-S-1-5-21-<GUID>' under the Microsoft task folder to autorun the relocated UevAppMonitor.exe at startup. The literal prefix plus the SID-style suffix is article-unique — legitimate OneDrive tasks do not embed an S-1-5-21 SID in their name. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2025/12/china-aligned-threat-group-uses-windows.html • https://cyberpress.org/apt-hackers-china-malware/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1053/005/
ATT&CKT1053.005 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
// LongNosedGoblin NosyDoor persistence — scheduled task with literal prefix "OneDrive Reporting Task-S-1-5-21-" registered under \Microsoft\ task folder.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated", "FileRenamed")
| where FolderPath has @"\Windows\System32\Tasks\Microsoft\"
| where FileName startswith "OneDrive Reporting Task-S-1-5-21-"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FolderPath, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName,
          InitiatingProcessAccountSid, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
// LongNosedGoblin NosyDoor — TaskScheduler 4698 with NosyDoor task-name prefix
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4698                                            // A scheduled task was created
| where EventData has "OneDrive Reporting Task-S-1-5-21-"          // article-specific prefix
| extend XmlData = parse_xml(EventData)
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, SubjectDomainName,
          SubjectLogonId, EventData
| order by TimeGenerated desc

NosyDoor dropper file artefacts in C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework AIInstallation High
Hunts the four-file drop the NosyDoor stage-1 dropper writes into C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework — SharedReg.dll, log.cached, netfxsbs9.hkf and UevAppMonitor.exe.config — names deliberately chosen to blend with legitimate SharedReg12.dll / netfxsbs12.hkf. None of these filenames are produced by genuine .NET Framework installation/servicing, so any user-mode write into that directory is a strong NosyDoor staging signal. Rationale: ESET names the exact four files the dropper writes to C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework (SharedReg.dll, log.cached, netfxsbs9.hkf, UevAppMonitor.exe.config) and explicitly notes their goal is to blend with the genuine SharedReg12.dll / netfxsbs12.hkf — so the '9' vs '12' suffix and the unsuffixed SharedReg.dll are themselves diagnostic. The 'co-occurrence ≥ 2 of N' threshold (skipping Sigma because that's a count predicate) keeps FP rate low while catching variants where one filename rotates. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2025/12/china-aligned-threat-group-uses-windows.html • https://www.securityweek.com/chinese-apt-longnosedgoblin-targeting-asian-governments/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1574/014/
ATT&CKT1036.005 T1574.014
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
// LongNosedGoblin NosyDoor dropper — writes a known set of artefacts into C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework so the relocated UevAppMonitor.exe loads SharedReg.dll via .config-driven AppDomainManager injection.
let NosyDoorFiles = dynamic([
    "SharedReg.dll",
    "log.cached",
    "netfxsbs9.hkf",            // typo of legitimate netfxsbs12.hkf
    "UevAppMonitor.exe.config",
    "UevAppMonitor.exe",         // staged copy from System32
    "error.txt"                  // dropped on stage-2 decryption errors
]);
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated", "FileModified", "FileRenamed")
| where FolderPath in~ (@"C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework", @"C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework64")
   or FolderPath endswith @"\Microsoft.NET\Framework"
   or FolderPath endswith @"\Microsoft.NET\Framework64"
| where FileName in~ (NosyDoorFiles)
// Exclude legitimate .NET servicing — the genuine SharedReg DLL is named SharedReg12.dll, not SharedReg.dll
| where not(InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("TrustedInstaller.exe", "msiexec.exe", "setup.exe") and InitiatingProcessFolderPath startswith @"C:\Windows\")
| summarize FileSet = make_set(FileName), FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp),
            Writers = make_set(InitiatingProcessFileName)
  by DeviceName, FolderPath, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| where array_length(FileSet) >= 2     // at least two of the four sibling artefacts
| order by LastSeen desc
// Sentinel equivalent via ASIM ImFileEvent — NosyDoor dropper artefact set
let NosyDoorFiles = dynamic(["SharedReg.dll","log.cached","netfxsbs9.hkf","UevAppMonitor.exe.config","UevAppMonitor.exe","error.txt"]);
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where TargetFilePath has @"\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework"
| where TargetFileName in~ (NosyDoorFiles)
| where not(ActingProcessName has_any ("TrustedInstaller.exe","msiexec.exe"))
| summarize FileSet = make_set(TargetFileName), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated),
            Writers = make_set(ActingProcessName)
          by DvcHostname, ActorUsername
| where array_length(FileSet) >= 2
| order by LastSeen desc
ENDPOINT

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement Actions on Objectives High
psexec / paexec / smbexec / wmic /node — service install over SMB from remote host.
ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
   or (FileName =~ "wmic.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "/node:")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688            // process-create (audit-policy required)
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: PsExec / SMB Lateral Movement Tooling Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d03
status: experimental
description: |
    PsExec, paexec, smbexec, csexec, remcom — admin-grade tooling that
    pivots over SMB. Pair with `wmic /node:` for the full cluster.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1021/002/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1569/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1021.002
    - attack.t1569.002
    - attack.lateral-movement
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_psexec:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\psexec.exe'
            - '\psexec64.exe'
            - '\psexesvc.exe'
            - '\paexec.exe'
            - '\csexec.exe'
            - '\remcom.exe'
            - '\smbexec.py'
    selection_wmic_remote:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\wmic.exe'
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '/node:'
    condition: selection_psexec or selection_wmic_remote
falsepositives:
    - Sysadmins running approved remote-management tooling — wrap with
      a known-admin-host watchlist
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")

CISA KEV: CVE-2025-59374 — ASUS Live Update Embedded Malicious Code Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-12-17 2 use cases 1 technique 3 kill-chain phases detected

ASUS Live Update contains an embedded malicious code vulnerability client were distributed with unauthorized modifications introduced through a supply chain compromise. The modified builds could cause devices meeting specific targeting conditions to perform unintended actions. The impacted product could be end-of-life (EoL) and/or end-of-service (EoS). Users should discontinue product utilization. Vendor: ASUS, Product: Live Update. Federal patch due: 2026-01-07.

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.

Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.

    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when

    vendor.
references:

author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02

    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'

falsepositives:

      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

| where FileName in~ ("Login Data","Cookies","logins.json","cookies.sqlite")

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1555/003/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1539
    - attack.t1555.003
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'
            - '\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\'
    selection_file:
        TargetFilename|endswith:
            - '\Login Data'
            - '\Cookies'
            - '\logins.json'
            - '\cookies.sqlite'
    filter_browser:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\chrome.exe'
            - '\msedge.exe'
            - '\firefox.exe'
            - '\brave.exe'
            - '\opera.exe'
    condition: selection_path and selection_file and not filter_browser
falsepositives:
    - Backup utilities reading user profile files
    - Browser sync agents from non-stock vendors
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where (Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Cookies*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\logins.json*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\cookies.sqlite*")
      AND NOT Filesystem.process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.process_name, Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.user
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where (Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Cookies*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\logins.json*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\cookies.sqlite*")
      AND NOT Filesystem.process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.process_name, Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.user
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent

| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"

   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"

          ParentProcessName

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Old AI Security vs Evo: Watch Agentic Security Replace Weeks of Manual Work

Snyk
2025-12-16 1 use case 1 technique 5 kill-chain phases detected

The rise of GenAI brings complex, non-deterministic security risks that traditional methods can't handle. Discover Evo by Snyk, the world's first agentic security orchestration system for AI-native defense.

ATT&CKT1195.002
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Actions on Objectives
Likely
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"

          NewProcessName, CommandLine

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)

    - attack.t1195

    category: process_creation

        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'

            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'

CISA KEV: CVE-2025-59718 — Fortinet Multiple Products Improper Verification of Cryptographic Signature Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-12-16 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

Fortinet FortiOS, FortiSwitchMaster, FortiProxy, and FortiWeb contain an improper verification of cryptographic signature vulnerability that may allow an unauthenticated attacker to bypass the FortiCloud SSO login authentication via a crafted SAML message. Please be aware that CVE-2025-59719 pertains to the same problem and is mentioned in the same vendor advisory. Ensure to apply all patches mentioned in the advisory. Vendor: Fortinet, Product: Multiple Products. Federal patch due: 2025-12-23.

Node.js process downloads payload via curl/wget (React2Shell SNOWLIGHT/VShell deployment) AIInstallation High
Detects post-exploitation behaviour following CVE-2025-55182 / CVE-2025-66478 RCE in React Server Components and Next.js App Router: the Next.js Node.js server process spawns curl, wget, bitsadmin, certutil, or a shell with a URL on the command line to stage second-stage tooling. Web-app Node.js servers virtually never spawn these binaries with URL arguments under normal operation, making this a high-fidelity post-RCE pivot. Rationale: Article explicitly names the vulnerable runtimes (`node`/`next` via React Server Components Flight protocol) — `npm list react` / `npm list next` confirm Node.js is the runtime under attack. Reliable RCE per the React Core Team disclosure means the FIRST post-exploit action is almost universally an `Ingress Tool Transfer` via the same Node process; we anchor on the (parent=node) + (child=download-utility) + (cmdline contains URL) triplet to keep FP low. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/facebook/react/security/advisories • https://vercel.com/changelog • https://www.wiz.io/blog/critical-vulnerability-in-react • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1190/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1105/
ATT&CKT1190 T1059 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Node-based build tools (e.g. node-gyp, electron-builder) invoking curl/wget to fetch native binaries during install
  • Internal CI/CD container images running `npm postinstall` scripts that call curl for license/manifest fetches
  • Developer workstations where a globally-installed npm package shells out to bitsadmin/certutil for caching
  • Headless e2e test runners (Playwright/Puppeteer) spawning shells that fetch test fixtures over HTTP
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("node-gyp","electron-builder","playwright install","puppeteer","npm-cli.js","yarn.js") or ProcessCommandLine has_any ("registry.npmjs.org","github.com/electron","playwright.azureedge.net","chromium-browser-snapshots"))
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Pull the full process ancestry for the Node.js parent (PID, listening port, working directory). Confirm the host is running Next.js App Router or a framework embedding `react-server-dom-*` at a vulnerable version (`npm list react`, `npm list next`). Capture the URL and any fetched file hash. Step 2 — Contain: Isolate the host in Defender/Falcon; block the destination URL/IP at the proxy; force-rotate any service credentials, cloud IAM tokens, and DB connection strings reachable from the Node process; snapshot memory if EDR supports it. Step 3 — Hunt: Pivot on the Node process SHA256/PID — search for other DeviceFileEvents writes from that PID (webshells, cron entries), DeviceNetworkEvents to the same C2, and identical TTPs on other Next.js fleet members. Patch to React 19.0.1/19.1.2/19.2.1 and Next.js per the article's table before returning the host to service.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe", "node")
| where FileName in~ ("curl.exe","wget.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","bash.exe","sh.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("http://","https://","ftp://","tftp://")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("node.exe", "node")
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("curl","wget","bitsadmin","certutil","powershell","pwsh","cmd.exe","bash","sh")
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("http://","https://","ftp://","tftp://")
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessId
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Next.js Node.js Spawns Download Utility (CVE-2025-66478 Post-Exploit)
id: 5d8c3b9e-4f1a-4e2b-bb53-1f7a9c6e2d11
description: Detects Node.js spawning curl/wget/bitsadmin/certutil/shell with a URL on the command line, a typical second-stage stager pattern after RCE via React Server Components / Next.js App Router (CVE-2025-55182, CVE-2025-66478).
references:
  - https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/critical-remote-code-execution-vulnerabilities-discovered-in-react-server-components-and-next-js
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2025/12/03
tags:

  - attack.t1059

logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  parent:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\node.exe'
  child_bins:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\curl.exe'
      - '\wget.exe'
      - '\bitsadmin.exe'
      - '\certutil.exe'

      - '\pwsh.exe'

      - '\sh.exe'
  url_args:

      - 'http://'
      - 'https://'
      - 'ftp://'
      - 'tftp://'
  condition: parent and child_bins and url_args

  - Application-bundled installers/updaters launched by Node.js build tooling

level: high

source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @ParentImage:(*\node.exe OR *\NODE.EXE OR *\Node.exe) @Image:(*\curl.exe OR *\Curl.exe OR *\CURL.EXE OR *\wget.exe OR *\WGET.EXE OR *\bitsadmin.exe OR *\certutil.exe OR *\powershell.exe OR *\PowerShell.exe OR *\cmd.exe OR *\bash.exe OR *\sh.exe) @CommandLine:(*http\:* OR *https\:* OR *ftp\:* OR *tftp\:*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ParentBaseFileName=/node(\.exe)?$/i ImageFileName=/\\(curl|wget|bitsadmin|certutil|powershell|pwsh|cmd|bash|sh)\.exe$/i
| regex("https?://|ftp://|tftp://", field=CommandLine)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())

Vulnerable React Server Components or Next.js App Router versions present in inventory AIReconnaissance High
Inventory pivot using Defender TVM to surface every host or repo image carrying a vulnerable react-server-dom-* package or affected Next.js App Router build (CVE-2025-55182 / CVE-2025-66478). Drives the patch list before exploitation rather than after — 39% of cloud environments are reportedly affected per Wiz research cited in the article. Rationale: Article enumerates the exact vulnerable package names (`react-server-dom-parcel/webpack/turbopack` 19.0.0–19.2.0) and Next.js version bands — TVM lookup against those strings and the two CVE IDs gives a clean, deterministic asset list. Cross-checked CVE IDs against the React Core Team security advisory and Wiz blog cited in the article. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/facebook/react/security/advisories • https://www.wiz.io/blog/critical-vulnerability-in-react • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-55182 • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-66478
ATT&CKT1190
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Containers carrying the vulnerable package but with the RSC code path disabled (not using App Router)
  • Dev-only hosts running canary builds that are never internet-exposed
  • Build artefacts cached on developer machines no longer in production rotation
  • Lockfile-pinned versions in archived repos that aren't deployed
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Confirm the host or repo is actually serving traffic (cross-reference with reverse-proxy / load-balancer config); identify whether the affected runtime is internet-exposed (Wiz reports 44% of Next.js instances are). Pull `npm list react` and `npm list next` from the host. Step 2 — Contain: For internet-exposed hosts on vulnerable versions, restrict ingress at the WAF/CDN until patched. For non-exposed but vulnerable assets, schedule emergency change. Step 3 — Hunt: Pivot to the Node.js + curl/wget detection on the same host list, plus DeviceNetworkEvents for unusual egress from `node.exe`, to confirm no prior exploitation. Approve the StepSecurity NPM Cooldown exception for the patched versions per the advisory.

         or (SoftwareName startswith "react-server-dom-" and SoftwareVersion has_any ("19.0.0","19.1.0","19.1.1","19.2.0"))

)

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — How Harden Runner Detected the Sha1-Hulud Supply Chain Attack in CNCF's Backstag
Installation. Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Sha1-Hulud npm Worm — Egress to bun.sh / oss.trufflehog.org / keychecker.trufflesecurity.com from npm/node context AIInstallation High
Hunts the article-specific network IOCs of the Sha1-Hulud Second Coming worm: any host (CI runner or developer workstation) reaching bun.sh (to drop the Bun runtime), oss.trufflehog.org (to fetch TruffleHog for secret scanning), or keychecker.trufflesecurity.com — initiated by node/npm/yarn/pnpm/bun/shell processes during an npm install lifecycle. These three destinations are the precise endpoints Harden Runner flagged as anomalous in the Backstage compromise; baseline npm installs never reach them. Rationale: bun.sh, oss.trufflehog.org and keychecker.trufflesecurity.com are the exact endpoints the article cites as anomalous in Backstage's E2E workflow — none of which appear in legitimate npm install network baselines. Cross-checked Unit 42, Wiz, Snyk, Semgrep, Sysdig and Datadog Security Labs write-ups, all confirming Bun runtime download from bun.sh and TruffleHog from oss.trufflehog.org are core stages of the Sha1-Hulud 2.0 worm. NpmContext predicate narrows to lifecycle-script-triggered egress. Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-2-0-ongoing-supply-chain-attack • https://snyk.io/blog/sha1-hulud-npm-supply-chain-incident/ • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2025/digging-for-secrets-sha1-hulud-the-second-coming-of-the-npm-worm/ • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/return-of-the-shai-hulud-worm-affects-over-25-000-github-repositories • https://securitylabs.datadoghq.com/articles/shai-hulud-2.0-npm-worm/

let _iocs = dynamic(["bun.sh","oss.trufflehog.org","keychecker.trufflesecurity.com"]);
let _npm_ctx = dynamic(["node.exe","node","npm.exe","npm-cli.js","yarn.exe","yarn","pnpm.exe","pnpm","bun.exe","bun","bash","sh","runner.listener.exe","runner.worker.exe"]);
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where RemoteUrl has_any (_iocs)
     or RemoteUrl endswith ".bun.sh"
     or RemoteUrl endswith ".trufflehog.org"
     or RemoteUrl endswith ".trufflesecurity.com"
| extend NpmContext = InitiatingProcessFileName in~ (_npm_ctx)
| summarize FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp),
            DistinctIocDomains = dcount(RemoteUrl),
            IocsHit = make_set(RemoteUrl, 10),
            InitiatingProcs = make_set(InitiatingProcessFileName, 10),
            SampleCmd = any(InitiatingProcessCommandLine),
            RemoteIPs = make_set(RemoteIP, 10),
            AnyNpmContext = max(tolong(NpmContext))
          by DeviceId, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| where DistinctIocDomains >= 2 or AnyNpmContext == 1
| order by FirstSeen desc

        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)

        | project TimeGenerated, Host = Computer, Src = ClientIP, User = "", Url = Name, Source = "DnsEvents" )
| summarize FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated),
            IocsHit = make_set(Url, 20),
            DistinctIocDomains = dcount(Url),
            Sources = make_set(Source)
          by Host, Src, User
| where DistinctIocDomains >= 1
| order by FirstSeen desc

Sha1-Hulud npm Worm — Self-Hosted GitHub Actions Runner Registration with Name 'SHA1HULUD' AIInstallation High
Detects the persistence stage of the Sha1-Hulud Second Coming worm: a process invocation containing the literal string 'SHA1HULUD' — the hard-coded runner name the worm passes to GitHub Actions' config.sh/config.cmd (or Runner.Listener) when registering the compromised host as a self-hosted runner via the leaked GitHub token. The literal is unique enough to ship as alerting with near-zero FP. Rationale: The runner name 'SHA1HULUD' is the highest-fidelity article-specific IOC — it is hard-coded by the worm and validated by Unit 42, Wiz, Sysdig, Snyk, Semgrep, and Datadog Security Labs as the persistent self-hosted-runner identity. The literal is unique enough that any command line containing it is essentially a confirmed compromise. Three casing variants emitted for Datadog case-sensitivity. Backup selection catches obfuscated variants by spotting GitHub Actions config scripts being run with --name + --token outside a legitimate _diag/_work directory. Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-2-0-ongoing-supply-chain-attack • https://snyk.io/blog/sha1-hulud-npm-supply-chain-incident/ • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/return-of-the-shai-hulud-worm-affects-over-25-000-github-repositories • https://blog.checkpoint.com/research/shai-hulud-2-0-inside-the-second-coming-the-most-aggressive-npm-supply-chain-attack-of-2025/

Sha1-Hulud npm Worm — Drop of setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js / discussion.yaml by node or shell AIInstallation Medium
Hunts the on-disk artefacts of the Sha1-Hulud Second Coming preinstall payload: creation of setup_bun.js (the loader that installs Bun), bun_environment.js (the main RC4-protected payload), or the malicious workflow .github/workflows/discussion.yaml that turns the host into a Discussion-triggered backdoor. File-creator is constrained to npm/node/yarn/pnpm/bun/shell to filter out unrelated files of the same names. Rationale: setup_bun.js and bun_environment.js are the loader and main payload filenames repeatedly cited by Unit 42, Sysdig, Semgrep, Aikido and Datadog Security Labs analyses of the Sha1-Hulud 2.0 worm. .github/workflows/discussion.yaml is the Discussion-event-triggered backdoor workflow planted on every infected repo by the same payload. Writer constraint to npm/node/yarn/pnpm/bun/shell processes filters out unrelated files with the same names being checked into a developer's git working tree. Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/ • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2025/digging-for-secrets-sha1-hulud-the-second-coming-of-the-npm-worm/ • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/return-of-the-shai-hulud-worm-affects-over-25-000-github-repositories • https://securitylabs.datadoghq.com/articles/shai-hulud-2.0-npm-worm/ • https://www.aikido.dev/blog/shai-hulud-strikes-again-hitting-zapier-ensdomains

description: |

// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09", "62ee164b9b306250c1172583f138c9614139264f889fa99614903c12755468d0", "f099c5d9ec417d4445a0328ac0ada9cde79fc37410914103ae9c609cbc0ee068", "cbb9bc5a8496243e02f3cc080efbe3e4a1430ba0671f2e43a202bf45b05479cd", "a3894003ad1d293ba96d77881ccd2071446dc3f65f434669b49b3da92421901a", "b74caeaa75e077c99f7d44f46daaf9796a3be43ecf24f2a1fd381844669da777", "dc67467a39b70d1cd4c1f7f7a459b35058163592f4a9e8fb4dffcbba98ef210c", "4b2399646573bb737c4969563303d8ee2e9ddbd1b271f1ca9e35ea78062538db", "d1829b4708126dcc7bea7437c04d1f10eacd4a16", "d60ec97eea19fffb4809bc35b91033b52490ca11", "3d7570d14d34b0ba137d502f042b27b0f37a59fa"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),

        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] NPM preinstall hook fetching Bun installer from bun.sh (Sha1-Hulud dropper)
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Sha1-Hulud: The Second Coming - Zapier, ENS Domains, and Other Prominent NPM Pac
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Bun/Node executing the Sha1-Hulud worm payload (setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js) · [LLM] Sha1-Hulud self-hosted GitHub Actions runner deployed under ~/.dev-env (SHA1HULUD)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Bun/Node initiating multi-cloud secret-manager enumeration burst (Sha1-Hulud aL0 harvest) · [LLM] Bun/Node bursty PUT to api.github.com /contents from infected host (Sha1-Hulud exfil) · Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
NPM preinstall hook fetching Bun installer from bun.sh (Sha1-Hulud dropper) AIDelivery High
Detects the Stage 1 dropper of the Sha1-Hulud: The Second Coming worm where a compromised npm package's preinstall script spawns curl|bash (Linux/macOS) or PowerShell irm|iex (Windows) to fetch the legitimate Bun installer from bun.sh. The parent process is node/npm/yarn/pnpm and the child fetches bun.sh/install or bun.sh/install.ps1 — a pattern reserved to the worm because legitimate developer Bun installs almost never come from inside an npm install lifecycle. Rationale: Anchored on the exact Stage 1 dropper command strings from the article — 'curl -fsSL https://bun.sh/install | bash' and 'powershell -c "irm bun.sh/install.ps1|iex"' — combined with the npm-lifecycle parent (node/npm/yarn/pnpm). Multiple vendors (Wiz, Microsoft, Red Canary, Elastic, Endor Labs) confirm bun.sh fetch from an npm preinstall context is article-specific because legitimate developer Bun installation almost never originates from an npm dependency tree. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-2-0-ongoing-supply-chain-attack • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2025/12/09/shai-hulud-2-0-guidance-for-detecting-investigating-and-defending-against-the-supply-chain-attack/ • https://redcanary.com/blog/threat-detection/shai-hulud-worm/ • https://www.endorlabs.com/learn/shai-hulud-2-malware-campaign-targets-github-and-cloud-credentials-using-bun-runtime • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.004 T1059.001 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer manually following a Bun upgrade tutorial in a terminal where the parent is a shell, not npm — filter on parent=shell not detected here, but volume should be small.
  • Internal SRE bootstrap script that legitimately installs Bun during container image build (e.g. Dockerfile RUN curl bun.sh/install) — parent is shell/Docker, not npm.
  • Engineering bootcamp / dev-env automation that installs Bun via Ansible/Chef and happens to chain through node.
  • Bun's own self-update path when an existing bun binary fetches its own installer.
  • CI job that explicitly declares 'curl bun.sh/install | bash' as a build step (visible in pipeline YAML).
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessParentFileName !in~ ("docker.exe","buildkitd","podman.exe","containerd")
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !has "\\Docker\\" and InitiatingProcessFolderPath !contains "/var/lib/docker/"
Response runbook
1. Triage: pull the full process tree for the alerting host — confirm the npm/node parent and the preceding `npm install <package>` invocation. Identify which package was being installed (check ~/.npm/_logs/, package-lock.json diff, or the CI job log). 2. Contain: kill the bun and node processes, quarantine ~/.bun/, ~/.dev-env/, and the npm cache; rotate all secrets exposed on the host (GITHUB_TOKEN, NPM_TOKEN, AWS/GCP/Azure credentials, ~/.netrc); revoke the user's GitHub PAT. 3. Hunt: look for child UC2/UC3 (bun_environment.js execution, SHA1HULUD runner) on the same host, then sweep all hosts that installed the suspect npm package version in the last 14d via SBOM or package-lock telemetry; check GitHub audit log for any newly-created public repos by the victim user.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("bun.sh/install", "bun.sh/install.ps1")
| where FileName in~ ("curl.exe","curl","bash","sh","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","node","npm.exe","npm","npm-cli.js","yarn.exe","yarn","pnpm.exe","pnpm","bun.exe","bun")
   or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("setup_bun.js","preinstall")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          ParentName = InitiatingProcessFileName,
          ParentCmd  = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ChildName  = FileName,
          ChildCmd   = ProcessCommandLine,
          SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("bun.sh/install", "bun.sh/install.ps1")
| where ActingProcessName matches regex @"(?i)(^|[\\/])(curl|bash|sh|powershell|pwsh|cmd)(\.exe)?$"
| where ParentProcessName matches regex @"(?i)(^|[\\/])(node|npm|yarn|pnpm|bun)(\.exe|-cli\.js)?$"
   or ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("setup_bun.js","preinstall")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine,
          ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine,
          SHA256

(source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @ParentImage:(*\\node.exe OR *\\Node.exe OR *\\NODE.EXE OR *\\npm.cmd OR *\\NPM.CMD OR *\\yarn.cmd OR *\\YARN.CMD OR *\\pnpm.cmd OR *\\PNPM.CMD OR *\\bun.exe OR *\\Bun.exe) @CommandLine:(*bun.sh/install* OR *bun.sh/install.ps1*)) OR (source:linux.auditd @auditd.type:EXECVE @process.parent.name:(node OR npm OR yarn OR pnpm OR bun) @process.command_line:*bun.sh/install*)

Bun/Node executing the Sha1-Hulud worm payload (setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js) AIInstallation High
Detects direct execution of the worm's named JavaScript artefacts by either the Node.js runtime (Stage 1 dropper) or the Bun runtime (Stage 2 10MB+ obfuscated payload). The filenames `setup_bun.js` and `bun_environment.js` are confirmed IOCs across multiple vendor write-ups and have no legitimate use outside this worm. Also catches the detached background fork on developer machines via the POSTINSTALL_BG env-var marker referenced in the article's deobfuscated code. Rationale: The filenames `setup_bun.js` (Stage 1 dropper) and `bun_environment.js` (Stage 2 10MB+ obfuscated payload) are exact, article-specific IOCs confirmed by Wiz, Orca, Elastic, Microsoft, Endor Labs, Trend Micro, and Snyk. They have no legitimate use outside this worm. The POSTINSTALL_BG marker (a recursion guard the malware sets when it forks the detached background copy on developer machines) is an extra high-confidence pivot from the article's deobfuscated source. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-2-0-ongoing-supply-chain-attack • https://orca.security/resources/blog/shai-hulud-npm-malware-wave-2/ • https://www.elastic.co/blog/shai-hulud-worm-2-0-updated-response • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2025/12/09/shai-hulud-2-0-guidance-for-detecting-investigating-and-defending-against-the-supply-chain-attack/ • https://snyk.io/blog/bun-based-stealer-hits-sap-cap-js-mbt-npm-packages/
ATT&CKT1059.007 T1027 T1564.003 T1546
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A defender / threat researcher detonating the sample in a sandbox VM with the legitimate filename for analysis.
  • Reverse-engineering / IR engineers running the deobfuscated payload offline against a controlled test repo.
  • A blue team purple-team exercise / tabletop replaying the IOC.
  • Filename collisions are essentially zero — no public open-source project ships a file named `bun_environment.js`.
Response runbook
1. Triage: this is a confirmed-bad IOC — treat as a true positive until disproven. Pull the process tree, the file path of the JS artefact, and the package.json that referenced it. Identify the compromised npm package and pinned version. 2. Contain: kill node + bun processes, quarantine the JS files, isolate the host from the network; immediately rotate GITHUB_TOKEN, ~/.netrc, NPM_TOKEN, ~/.npmrc, and any AWS/Azure/GCP credentials that were in scope; revoke all PATs for the affected user. 3. Hunt: pivot to UC4 (cloud-secret enumeration) and UC3 (SHA1HULUD runner) on the same host; review the user's GitHub account for newly-created public repos with description 'Sha1-Hulud: The Second Coming'; audit npm publish history for any package owned by the user in the last 24h; sweep the org SBOM / lockfile telemetry for the same package version across all hosts.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("setup_bun.js","bun_environment.js")
| where FileName in~ ("node.exe","node","bun.exe","bun")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| extend HasBgMarker = InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "POSTINSTALL_BG"
           or ProcessCommandLine has "POSTINSTALL_BG"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          ParentName = InitiatingProcessFileName,
          ParentCmd  = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          GrandparentName = InitiatingProcessParentFileName,
          HasBgMarker, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("setup_bun.js","bun_environment.js")
| where ActingProcessName matches regex @"(?i)(^|[\\/])(node|bun)(\.exe)?$"
| extend HasBgMarker = ActingProcessCommandLine has "POSTINSTALL_BG"
           or ParentProcessCommandLine has "POSTINSTALL_BG"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine,
          ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine,
          HasBgMarker, SHA256

Sha1-Hulud self-hosted GitHub Actions runner deployed under ~/.dev-env (SHA1HULUD) AIInstallation High
Detects the worm's Stage 4 persistence: download of the official actions-runner-linux-x64 archive to a hidden $HOME/.dev-env/ directory and registration of a self-hosted runner with the literal name 'SHA1HULUD'. The runner is paired with an injected discussion.yaml workflow that lets any GitHub user trigger arbitrary shell execution via the runner. The .dev-env folder name and SHA1HULUD literal are confirmed cross-vendor IOCs. Rationale: Two complementary IOCs from the article's verbatim code block: (a) `mkdir -p $HOME/.dev-env/` followed by curl of `actions-runner-linux-x64-2.330.0.tar.gz` → file write of an actions-runner archive under any `.dev-env` directory; (b) the literal runner name 'SHA1HULUD' passed to Runner.Listener / config.sh. Wiz, Microsoft, and Red Canary all publish the same Runner.Listener+SHA1HULUD pivot. The `.dev-env` directory has no legitimate use — GitHub's official self-hosted runner guidance places runners under `actions-runner/` in $HOME. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-2-0-ongoing-supply-chain-attack • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2025/12/09/shai-hulud-2-0-guidance-for-detecting-investigating-and-defending-against-the-supply-chain-attack/ • https://redcanary.com/blog/threat-detection/shai-hulud-worm/ • https://www.elastic.co/blog/shai-hulud-worm-2-0-updated-response • https://cycode.com/blog/shai-hulud-second-coming-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1543 T1546 T1078.004 T1554
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • An engineer manually testing a personal GitHub self-hosted runner and naming the test instance 'SHA1HULUD' for blue-team simulation — verify via change ticket.
  • Internal red-team exercise replaying the IOC with the literal name as part of detection validation.
  • Incident-response sandbox restoring an infected runner to a forensic VM.
  • Legitimate self-hosted runner installations land in $HOME/actions-runner/ (GitHub default), $HOME/_work/, or /opt/actions-runner/ — never under `.dev-env`.
Suppression filter
| where ActingProcessCommandLine !has "--check" and ActingProcessCommandLine !has "--remove"
Response runbook
1. Triage: pull the full runner config from the host (`config.sh --check` output, the .runner JSON file, .credentials file under the runner dir). Identify which GitHub repo/org the runner is registered against and which workflows reference 'self-hosted' or label 'SHA1HULUD'. 2. Contain: stop the Runner.Listener service (`systemctl stop actions.runner.*` on Linux, `Stop-Service` on Windows), remove the runner from GitHub via UI/API (`DELETE /repos/{owner}/{repo}/actions/runners/{id}`), delete $HOME/.dev-env/ and any injected `.github/workflows/discussion.yaml`; rotate the PAT used to register the runner. 3. Bun/Node initiating multi-cloud secret-manager enumeration burst (Sha1-Hulud aL0 harvest) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects the Stage 3 `collectAndExfiltrate()` harvest where the worm's Bun-hosted payload fans out to AWS Secrets Manager, GCP Secret Manager and Azure Key Vault in rapid succession. The detection looks for a single bun/node process (or a single CI identity) hitting two or more of the three cloud-secret API endpoints inside a short window — behaviour that legitimate CI/CD jobs almost never exhibit because they're scoped to one provider. Rationale: The article's deobfuscated `collectAndExfiltrate()` explicitly lists `await new AWSClient().listAndRetrieveAllSecrets()`, `await new GCPClient().listAndRetrieveAllSecrets()` and `await new AzureClient().listAndRetrieveAllSecrets()` in sequence. The article-specific behaviour is the simultaneous fan-out across all three clouds from a single node/bun process — legitimate CI jobs are nearly always scoped to one provider. Microsoft and Wiz both call out this multi-cloud sweep as a high-signal pivot. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2025/12/09/shai-hulud-2-0-guidance-for-detecting-investigating-and-defending-against-the-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-2-0-ongoing-supply-chain-attack • https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/25/k/shai-hulud-2-0-targets-cloud-and-developer-systems.html • https://www.endorlabs.com/learn/shai-hulud-2-malware-campaign-targets-github-and-cloud-credentials-using-bun-runtime
ATT&CKT1552.005 T1555.006 T1078.004 T1526
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Multi-cloud SRE tooling like HashiCorp Vault Agent or external-secrets-operator that legitimately syncs from all three providers — usually runs as a known service identity (kubernetes ServiceAccount), not bun/node.
  • Crossplane or Terraform Cloud workspaces that touch multiple providers — parent process is terraform/crossplane, not bun.
  • Disaster-recovery rehearsal that intentionally enumerates secrets across all clouds — should be ticketed.
  • Multi-cloud cost-management or CSPM scanner (e.g. Wiz, Lacework agent) — runs under its own daemon user, not a developer/CI account.
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessParentFileName !in~ ("vault-agent","external-secrets","crossplane","terraform","vault.exe")
| where AccountName !has "vault-" and AccountName !has "crossplane-" and AccountName !has "externalsecrets-"
Response runbook
1. Triage: identify the specific bun/node PID and its parent chain — did it descend from `npm install`, a GitHub Actions runner step, or a developer terminal? Pull the AWS/GCP/Azure audit logs for the same identity in the 30-minute window and count secrets touched. 2. Contain: revoke the IAM role / service principal / GCP service account; rotate every secret returned by ListSecrets/AccessSecretVersion in that window (assume all read); block egress to the worm's GitHub exfil repos at the proxy. 3. Hunt: correlate with UC2 (bun_environment.js exec) on the same host; check GitHub for any newly-created public repos by the user with description 'Sha1-Hulud: The Second Coming'; pull CloudTrail/GCP/Azure logs for any subsequent use of stolen secrets (RDS snapshot copy, IAM user creation, S3 GetObject on protected buckets) in the 24h after the harvest.

Bun/Node bursty PUT to api.github.com /contents from infected host (Sha1-Hulud exfil) AIActions on Objectives High
Detects the Stage 3 exfiltration channel where the worm uses the stolen GITHUB_TOKEN to call `PUT /repos/{owner}/{repo}/contents/{path}` and upload `system.json`, `environment.json`, `secrets.json`, `trufflehog-findings.json`, `npm.json` to a newly-created public repo described 'Sha1-Hulud: The Second Coming'. Correlates a recent setup_bun.js/bun_environment.js execution on the host with a burst of api.github.com connections from the same bun/node runtime within 1 hour. Rationale: The article's deobfuscated `collectAndExfiltrate()` shows the exfil call sequence: `githubClient.saveContents("system.json"...)`, `environment.json`, `secrets.json`, `trufflehog-findings.json`, `npm.json` — all PUTs to `/repos/{owner}/{repo}/contents/{path}` on api.github.com using the stolen token. The temporal correlation (infected→api.github.com burst within 1h from same bun/node) is article-specific because npm/yarn install almost never makes 5+ api.github.com requests from the bun runtime in such a tight window. Wiz, Microsoft, and Red Canary all confirm the Contents-API exfil channel and the random-UUID repo naming with the worm-tagged description. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2025/12/09/shai-hulud-2-0-guidance-for-detecting-investigating-and-defending-against-the-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-2-0-ongoing-supply-chain-attack • https://redcanary.com/blog/threat-detection/shai-hulud-worm/ • https://socradar.io/blog/shai-hulud-the-second-coming-npm-campaign/ • https://orca.security/resources/blog/shai-hulud-npm-malware-wave-2/
ATT&CKT1567.001 T1102.002 T1213.003 T1071.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate `gh` CLI usage by a developer right after installing a package — but the parent process is gh, not bun, and the 5-hit threshold tied to bun_environment.js exec narrows.
  • Renovate / Dependabot bot updates that PR multiple files to github.com — but those run from github.com's own infra, not a victim host running bun_environment.js.
  • Storybook/Vercel build hook syncing package metadata to GitHub via API — but doesn't follow setup_bun.js infection trigger.
  • CI step that uses octokit to commit build artefacts back to a repo — pre-baseline the CI hosts that do this and add to allow-list.
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessParentFileName !in~ ("gh.exe","gh","renovate","dependabot")
| where AccountName !endswith "-bot" and AccountName !has "renovate" and AccountName !has "dependabot"
Response runbook
1. Triage: identify the GITHUB_TOKEN scope used by the bun process (env var dump in the process tree, or pull from the AAD/GitHub audit log for token issuance just before InfectionTime); enumerate the API endpoints hit — if any are `POST /user/repos` or `PUT /repos/{user}/{uuid}/contents/secrets.json`, treat as confirmed exfil. 2. Contain: revoke the GITHUB_TOKEN and every PAT on the victim's GitHub account, force-rotate npm/cloud secrets the harvest could have exposed (see UC2/UC4 runbooks), block api.github.com egress for the host until cleared. 3. Hunt: list all repos owned by the victim user via `GET /user/repos?affiliation=owner` and filter to repos with description matching 'Sha1-Hulud: The Second Coming' — delete and report to GitHub Trust & Safety; pivot to other org members whose tokens may have been on the same compromised CI runner; check GitHub audit log for `git.push` and `repo.create` events by the victim in the 1h window.
let Lookback = 24h;
let Infection = DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(Lookback)
    | where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("bun_environment.js","setup_bun.js")
    | summarize InfectionTime = min(Timestamp) by DeviceName;
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(Lookback)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("bun.exe","bun","node.exe","node")
| where RemoteUrl has "api.github.com"
| join kind=inner Infection on DeviceName
| where Timestamp between (InfectionTime .. InfectionTime + 1h)
| summarize ApiHits = count(),
            FirstHit = min(Timestamp),
            LastHit  = max(Timestamp),

            by DeviceName, AccountName = InitiatingProcessAccountName,
               InitiatingProcessFileName, InfectionTime
| where ApiHits >= 5
| extend DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', FirstHit, InfectionTime)
| project DeviceName, AccountName, InfectionTime, FirstHit, LastHit, DelaySec, ApiHits, InitiatingProcessFileName, Endpoints
| order by FirstHit desc

| extend DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', FirstHit, InfectionTime)
| order by FirstHit desc
(source:windows.sysmon @EventID:3 @Image:(*\\bun.exe OR *\\Bun.exe OR *\\node.exe OR *\\Node.exe OR *\\NODE.EXE) @DestinationHostname:api.github.com) OR (source:linux.auditd @process.name:(bun OR node) @network.destination.domain:api.github.com)

| _time >= InfectionTime AND _time <= InfectionTime + 3600000
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, InfectionTime], function=[count(as=ApiHits), min(_time, as=FirstApi)])

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files

Critical

Supply Chain Security Alert: eslint-config-prettier Package Shows Signs of Compromise

StepSecurity
2025-12-15 11 use cases 5 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

We are currently investigating a potential supply chain security incident involving the eslint-config-prettier npm package. This widely-used package, which helps developers maintain consistent code formatting by turning off ESLint rules that conflict with Prettier, appears to have had multiple versions published with suspicious modifications.

CVEsCVE-2025-54313
ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002 T1555.003 T1566 T1566.001
